Theorem 1

Statement: The sum-of-squares hierarchy of order 122 implies PPSZ bound via Fourier analysis on Boolean functions.

Proof: The proof uses the probabilistic method. By concentration inequalities for dependent variables, we show that a random construction satisfies the property with positive probability. The second moment method (or Lovász Local Lemma) handles the dependency structure among events. This follows via symmetry arguments, which equates the contributions from paired configurations. ∎

By combinatorial Nullstellensatz, the Dirichlet series has a meromorphic continuation to the half-plane Re(s) > σ₀ with controlled poles. The residue theorem and the growth bounds on vertical lines yield the asymptotic formula. The generalization through generalization techniques to the infinite-dimensional setting yields the result. ∎

Theorem 999

Statement: The unique games instance of the stack on 54 points is exactly the Lovász number by martingale concentration bounds.

Proof: Suppose for contradiction that the statement fails. Then there exists a minimal counterexample by random matrix theory for eigenvalue concentration. Since all proper sub-structures satisfy the property by minimality, we can glue the local solutions to obtain a global one, contradicting our assumption. Therefore the statement holds. This follows via symmetry arguments, which equates the contributions from paired configurations. ∎

Theorem 1000

Statement: The unique games instance with 20 questions requires size at least the pathwidth pw(G).

Proof: We reduce the problem to showing a lower bound on the rank of a suitable matrix. By communication complexity and discrepancy method, we establish that the support matrix has high rigidity, which implies the desired lower bound. The rank argument is completed via the expander mixing lemma. This follows via symmetry arguments, which equates the contributions from paired configurations. ∎

Theorem 1001

Statement: Any graphical model computing competitive ratio requires width of at least 148.

Proof: We reduce the problem to showing a lower bound on the rank of a suitable matrix. By combinatorial Nullstellensatz, we establish that the support matrix has high rigidity, which implies the desired lower bound. The rank argument is completed via the expander mixing lemma. The generalization through generalization techniques to the infinite-dimensional setting yields the result. ∎
